  1. Consequence: What is an effect of the belief or the relationship defined by the belief that can be leveraged for something productive? Is there any positive benefit or by-product of this belief?
  1. 后果（Consequence）： 这个信念、或该信念所定义的关系，有何效应可被借力于有建设性的事？这个信念有无任何正面益处或副作用？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Another Outcome: What other related outcome could be more useful and productive than the one stated or implied by the belief? If the belief focuses on avoiding or going away from a problem, what desired state could it be redirected toward?
  1. 另一种结果（Another Outcome）： 有什么其他相关的、比信念所陈述或暗示者更有用、更具建设性的结果？若这信念聚焦于回避或逃离某个问题，它可转向什么所欲的状态？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Chunk Down: What smaller elements or chunks are implied by the belief but have a richer or more positive relationship than the ones stated in the belief? What is true or not true for the part that might be different from the larger whole?
  1. 向下归类（Chunk Down）： 这信念隐含了哪些更小的元素或块，却有着比信念中所陈述者更丰富或更正向的关系？对那个部分而言，为真或不为真的东西，可能与更大的整体有所不同？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Chunk Up: What larger classification of the elements making up the belief can lead to a richer or more productive result? What is true or not true for the larger classification that might be different from the parts which make it up?
  1. 向上归类（Chunk Up）： 构成这信念的各元素，其更大的分类是什么，能导向更丰富或更具建设性的结果？对那更大的分类而言，为真或不为真的东西，可能与构成它的各部分有所不同？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Analogy: What is some other relationship which is analogous to that defined by the belief statement (a metaphor for some aspect of the belief), but which has more positive or productive implications or can stimulate new possibilities?
  1. 类比（Analogy）： 有什么其他关系，类似于这信念陈述所定义的（即某信念面向的隐喻），却带有更正面向、更具建设性，或可激发新的可能性？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Counter Example: What is an example or experience that is an exception to the premise or rule defined by the belief/ generalization that can bring more nuance and flexibility?
  1. 反例（Counter Example）： 有什么例子或经验，是这信念/概括所定义的预设或规则之例外，从而能带来更多细微差别与弹性？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Hierarchy of Criteria: What is a potentially more important or impactful criterion that has not yet been considered which could bring a significant change in perceptions or priorities?
  1. 准则的层级（Hierarchy of Criteria）： 有什么尚未被考量、却可能更重要或更具影响力的准则，能带来认知或优先序上的显著改变？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Apply to Self: What happens when you view the belief statement or its consequences through the filter of belief or its criteria? Can the belief statement itself be challenged or confirmed according to the generalization or criteria defined by the belief? Is the statement (or ongoing behavior/ intent of the speaker) an example of the belief or its criteria?
  1. 运用于自身（Apply to Self）： 当你透过这信念或其准则的滤镜去看待该信念陈述（或其后果）时，会发生什么？这信念陈述本身，能否依该信念所定义的概括或准则而被质疑或确认？这陈述（或说话者持续的行为/意图），是这信念或其准则的一个例子吗？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Change Frame Size: What is a longer (or shorter) time frame, a larger number or smaller number of people, or a bigger or smaller perspective that would change the implications of the belief to be something more flexible or positive?
  1. 改变框架大小（Change Frame Size）： 一个更长（或更短）的时间框架、更多或更少的人数、或更大或更小的视角，会改变这信念的意涵，使其变得更灵活或更正面向吗？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Model of the World: What is a different model of the world (i.e., role or identity) that would provide a very different perspective on the belief that could open up other more productive or resourceful possibilities or choices?
  1. 世界观（Model of the World）： 有什么不同的世界观（即角色或身份），能对这信念提供一个非常不同的视角，从而开启其他更具建设性或更有资源的物力或选择？

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. Reality Strategy: What cognitive perceptions of the world are necessary to have built this belief? How would one need to perceive the world in order for this belief to be true? What are the specific perceptions (and qualities of perception) and assumptions upon
  1. 现实策略（Reality Strategy）： 构建此信念，需要什么对世界的认知感知？要让此信念为真，一个人须如何感知世界？该信念建于其上、并持续使其到位的具体感知（及感知的特质）与假设是什么，以及那些持续使其到位的东西？那些又如何被调整，以增添更多可能性？

which this belief has been built, and which continue to hold it in place? How might those be adjusted to add more possibilities?

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

  1. 元框架（Meta Frame）： 有什么关于此信念的信念，能改变或丰富该信念的感知或意涵？
  1. Meta Frame: What is a belief about this belief that could change or enrich the perception or implication of the belief?"

______________________________________________________________

______________________________________________________________

运用"天才的模式"组合语言的魔力模式，以转化阻抗Combining Sleight of Mouth Patterns Using Patterns of Genius to Transform Resistance

Learning is finding out that you already know. Doing is demonstrating that you know it. Teaching is reminding others that they know just as well as you. —Richard Bach
学习，是发现自己早已知道。践行，是证明你知道。教学，是提醒他人：他们也和你一样知道。——理查德·巴赫（Richard Bach）

The examples of Erickson's strategic use of Sleight of Mouth so far have focused on how he applied a single pattern. Sleight of Mouth patterns can be even more effective when they are combined and guided by the various Patterns of Genius. A great example of Erickson's genius in combining various macro strategies of genius with the micro strategies of Sleight of Mouth is the case of how he helped an elderly illiterate woman learn to read and write (Collected Papers I, pp. 197-201). Erickson describes the situation in the following way.

艾瑞克森对语言的魔力的策略性运用，迄今所举的例子，焦点都放在他如何运用单一模式。而当语言的魔力模式被组合、并由各种"天才的模式"引导时，会更为有效。艾瑞克森将各种宏观的天才策略与语言的魔力的微观策略相结合的才华，一个绝佳的例子，是他如何帮助一位不识字的老年妇女学会读写的案例（《论文集 I》，pp. 197-201）。艾瑞克森如下描述这情形。

A 70-year-old woman born in a rural community had not been allowed to attend school since her parents did not believe in education for women. At the age of 14, she married a youth of 16, whose formal education was limited to his signature of signing checks and "figgering." The bride was pleased with her husband's greater education and resolved to have him teach her, since she resented her lack of schooling. This hope did not materialize. During the next six years, she was kept busy with farm work and pregnancies. She did learn to "figger" excellently but only mentally, since it was apparently impossible for her to learn to inscribe numerals. Neither was she able to learn to sign her name.

一位出身乡间社区的七十岁妇女，自小不被允许上学，因为她的父母不认同女性受教育。十四岁那年，她嫁给了一个十六岁的青年，那青年的正规教育仅限于会签支票和"算数"。新娘对丈夫更高的学历感到满意，并决心让他教自己，因为她对自己没受过教育心怀怨怼。这希望并未实现。在接下来的六年里，她忙于农活与接连怀孕。她确实把"算数"学得极好，但仅限于心算，因为她显然无法学会写出数字。她也没能学会签自己的名字。

At the age of 20, she hit upon the idea of furnishing room and board for the local rural schoolteacher with the intention of receiving, in return for reduced rates, the much desired instruction in reading and writing. Each school year for the next 50 years, she made and kept her agreement. The teachers hopefully began the attempt and finally, and soon, others only after prolonged labor, abandoned the task of teaching her as hopeless. As the community grew, the number of teachers increased until she was boarding year after year a total of four. None succeeded, despite the sincerity of her desire and the honesty of their effort... Each time she was given a lesson, she invariably developed a state of mental blankness after the manner of a seriously frightened small child or a state of frantic disorganized efforts to please that led to a total impasse.

二十岁时，她想出一个主意：为当地的乡村教师提供食宿，以期以较低的食宿费作为交换，换来她梦寐以求的读写指导。在之后五十年的每一年学年里，她都信守着这约定。教师们满怀希望地开始尝试，有的很快放弃，有的在漫长辛苦后才放弃，都将教她视为无望之举。随着社区发展，教师人数增加，直到她每年接待的寄宿教师总数达到四位。尽管她求学的意愿真诚、教师们也竭尽全力，却无一成功……每次给她上课，她必定陷入一种类似受惊小孩的精神空白状态，或是陷入一种为讨好而疯狂、混乱的努力，最终走向彻底的僵局。

In spite of all of the complete lack of progress, the woman remained adamant about wanting to learn to read and write. According to Erickson, the woman was actually quite intelligent and even frequently gave strangers that impression through her conversation that she had a college education.

尽管全然没有进展，那女子仍执意想学会读写。据艾瑞克森说，那女子其实相当聪明，甚至常通过交谈给陌生人留下她受过大学教育的印象。

Knowing the woman's history, Erickson told her he could help her, boldly claiming that she would be reading and writing within three weeks' time "without being taught anything that she did not already know and had known for a long time." In fact, the woman was told that she would have to "obey completely and without argument every instruction given her, provided that it was something she already knew how to do and had already learned about a long time ago." The woman was puzzled but agreed.

了解这女子的过往后，艾瑞克森告诉她自己能帮她，并大胆宣称，她会在三周内学会读写，"而不必被教任何她尚未知道、或很久以前就已知道的东西"。事实上，那女子被告知，她必须"完全、毫无异议地服从每一条给她的指令，条件是：那必须是她早已会做、且很久以前就已学会的事。"女子虽困惑，仍同意了。

Erickson gave the woman some paper and a pencil and instructed her to "just pick up the pencil any old way. Hold it in your hand any old way. You and I know you can do that. Any baby can pick up a pencil in any old way." After the woman picked up the pencil Erickson continued, "O.K. Now make a mark on the paper; any old scribbling mark—like a baby that can't write makes. Just any old, crooked mark! That's something you don't even have to learn."

艾瑞克森给了那女子一些纸和一支铅笔，指示她"就用随便什么方式拿起铅笔。用随便什么方式握在手里。你我知道你能做到。随便哪个婴儿都能随便怎么拿起一支铅笔。"女子拿起铅笔后，艾瑞克森继续说："好。现在在纸上做个记号；随便什么涂鸦的记号——就像不会写字的婴儿那样。就随便来个歪歪扭扭的记号！那是你连学都不用学就会的。"

As the woman began to scribble Erickson said, "OK. Now make a straight mark on the paper, like you make with a nail when you want to saw a board straight or with a stick when you make a row in the garden. You can make it short or long, or straight up and down, or just lying down. Now make a mark like the hole in a doughnut; then two marks like the halves of the doughnut when you break the doughnut in half. Now make two slanted marks; one like one side of the gable roof of a barn and the other like the other side. And now poke the pencil at the paper and make just a little spot."

当女子开始涂鸦，艾瑞克森说："好。现在在纸上画一道直的记号，就像你想把木板锯直时用钉子划的，或是你在园子里划线时用棍子划的。你可以画得短或长，竖直或横躺。现在画一个像甜甜圈中间的洞那样的记号；然后画两个记号，像你把甜甜圈掰成两半时的两半。现在画两道斜的记号；一道像谷仓山墙屋顶的一边，另一道像另一边。现在用铅笔在纸上戳一下，点一个小点。"

Erickson then went on to say, "Now all those marks you can make different sizes, in different places on the paper, in different order, and even one on top of the other or one next to another." He then instructed here to go home and practice "making those marks that you made and can make again any old time."

艾瑞克森接着说："现在，所有这些记号，你都能做成不同大小、在纸上不同位置、以不同顺序，甚至一个叠在另一个上、或一个挨着另一个。"接着他指示她回家练习"做出那些记号——你能做、且随时都能再做的记号"。

When she came back the following day, Erickson learned that she had spent approximately two hours "making marks." Telling the woman that the only difference between a pile of lumber and a house was that a completed house was the lumber "merely put together," Erickson began having her "put the marks together in 2's and 3's in different ways." With "a little judicious maneuvering and indirect guidance" from Erickson it was possible to "secure from among the various combinations she made the complete alphabet printed in block form."

次日她回来时，艾瑞克森得知她花了约两个小时"做记号"。艾瑞克森告诉她，一堆木料与一栋房子的唯一区别，在于完工的房子只是把木料"简单组合在一起"，于是他开始让她"以不同方式、把记号两个三个地组合起来"。靠着艾瑞克森"审慎的巧劲与间接引导"，得以"从她做出的各种组合里，取出完整的大写字母表"。

Erickson then picked up a newspaper and a child's book and pointed out how the letters in the publications were similar to the "combinations of marks" she had made. She was quite startled and excited to realize that the publications were using "her marks."

艾瑞克森接着拿起一份报纸和一本童书，指出其中的字母与她做出的"记号组合"多么相似。当她意识到那些出版物用的是"她的记号"时，既惊又喜。

Erickson next had the woman move from "letter building" to "word building" and then "naming" the word she had constructed. Erickson never mentioned "writing" or "reading." Instead, he told her, "Take some of these straight or crooked lines and build me another letter. Now build me a few letters alongside each other and name the word." Erickson told the woman that, just like all the farm animals had names, all these "word critters" had names. He emphasized the importance of learning the right "name" for each word, just like a child learns the names of things in its environment.

艾瑞克森接着让那女子从"组字母"进展到"组词"，再为她所拼出的词"命名"。艾瑞克森从未提及"写"或"读"。相反，他告诉她："拿这些直的或弯的线，再给我组一个字母。现在挨着组几个字母，把那个词念出来。"艾瑞克森对女子说，就像所有的农场动物都有名字，这些"词儿"也都有名字。他强调学会每个词正确的"名字"之重要，就像小孩学习周遭事物的名字那样。

According to Erickson, the task of "naming" the words became most fascinating to her.

据艾瑞克森说，"命名"这些词，成了让她最着迷的事。

The final step was to have the woman discover that "naming words is just like talking." He did this by having her "build" a series of words, apparently chosen at random, and then "set them on this straight line." She was then instructed to name all of the words on the line. The words were: "Get going Ma, and put some grub on the table." As she completed "naming" the words, she declared with astonishment, "Why, that's what Pa always says – it is just like talking." The transition from "talking words" to "reading words" was easily made and, according to Erickson, "within three weeks' time, she was spending every spare minute with her dictionary and a Reader's Digest." He reported that for the remainder of her life she was "a most prolific reader and frequent letter writer to her children and grandchildren."

最后一步，是让那女子发现"给词命名，就像说话一样"。他让她"拼"出一系列看似随机选出的词，然后"把它们排在这条直线上"。接着他指示她把直线上的所有词都念出来。那些词是："赶紧的妈，往桌上弄点吃的。"当她完成"命名"这些词，她惊讶地宣称："哎呀，这正是爸老说的话——这就跟说话一样。"从"说出来的词"过渡到"读出来的词"轻而易举，而且据艾瑞克森说，"三周之内，她把每一点空闲都花在字典和《读者文摘》上。"他报告说，在她余生中，她成了"极爱阅读的人，并频繁给儿女和孙辈写信"。

对这例子的反思Reflections on the Example

In this example, Erickson essentially combines the patterns of genius of "moving back and forth between different chunk sizes and levels of thinking" with the "use of metaphors and analogies" and "shifting perspectives." To do so, he applies the process of "pacing and leading" using an ingenious combination of Meta Frame and Consequence, Chunking Down and Up, Analogy and Redefine.

在此例中，艾瑞克森实质上将"在不同归类大小与思考层次间来回移动"的天才模式，与"运用隐喻和类比"以及"转换视角"相结合。为此，他运用"先跟后带"的过程，巧妙组合了元框架与后果、向下归类与向上归类、类比与重新定义。

Erickson begins by establishing the Meta Frame that the woman would not be "taught anything that she did not already know and had known for a long time." He then set up the self-reinforcing Consequence that the woman was obligated to "do what she already knew how to do." So, if she did it, she already knew how to do it. And, if she already knew how to do it, she had to do it.

艾瑞克森先确立起元框架：那女子不会被"教任何她尚未知道、或很久以前就已知道的东西"。接着他设下自我强化的后果：女子有义务"去做她早已会做的事"。于是，如果她做了，她早就知道怎么做；而如果她早就知道怎么做，她就非做不可。

By having her hold the pencil "any old way" and poking and scribbling on the paper, Erickson was clearly Chunking Down "writing" into simply making different types of marks on a piece of paper "like any baby could do." He then has the woman Chunk Up combinations of the marks into "letters," "words" and, ultimately, sentences.

让那女子"随便怎么"握笔、在纸上戳戳涂涂，艾瑞克森清楚地将"书写"向下归类为只是在纸上做各类记号，"就像任何婴儿都能做的"。接着他让女子把记号的组合向上归类为"字母""词"，最终成为句子。

As he does, he uses familiarizing Analogies such as "marking a board with a nail," "making a row in a garden with a stick," a "doughnut," "the gable roof of a barn," "building a house out of lumber" and "word critters" being like farm animals. He also compares the tasks he is asking the woman to do to be like something a "baby" or a "child" can easily do.

在此过程中，他运用让人感到熟悉的类比，例如"用钉子在木板上划线""用棍子在园子里划线""甜甜圈""谷仓的山墙屋顶""用木料盖房子"，以及"词儿"就像农场动物。他还将要求女子做的任务，比作"婴儿"或"小孩"都能轻易做到的事。

Erickson Redefines "writing" and "reading" to "making marks," "word building," "naming" words and ultimately "talking words." His remark that the "letters" in the newspaper and the book were similar to the "combinations of marks" the woman had made creates an important shift in perspective – the publications are using marks that she knows how to make rather than her trying to learn the letters they are using. As a result, the woman discovers that she "already knows" how to read and write.

艾瑞克森将"书写"和"阅读"重新定义为"做记号""组词""给词命名"，最终成为"说出词"。他提到报纸和书中的"字母"与女子做出的"记号组合"相似，这制造了一个重要的视角转换——那些出版物用的是她会做的记号，而不是让她去学它们所用的字母。结果，那女子发现自己"早已会"读写。

It is interesting to note that the combination of patterns – chunking down, redefining, using analogies and chunking up – Erickson applied has many similarities to the strategy Wolfgang Mozart used to compose music (see Strategies of Genius Volume I, pp. 224-239) and Leonardo da Vinci used to create his paintings and machines (see Strategies of Genius Volume III, pp. 300-340).

有趣的是，艾瑞克森所运用的这一模式组合——向下归类、重新定义、运用类比、向上归类——与沃尔夫冈·莫扎特作曲所用的策略（见《天才的策略 第一卷》，pp. 224-239）、以及列奥纳多·达·芬奇创作绘画与机械所用的策略（见《天才的策略 第三卷》，pp. 300-340）有许多相似之处。

It also has striking similarities to the process that Pulitzer Prize winning classical music composer Michael Colgrass developed to teach people of all ages to compose and score a collective piece of music in 45 minutes (see Strategies of Genius Volume I, pp. 251-256). The process involves having a group of people "start making any old sounds." Colgrass would then ask one of them to go to a blackboard and to "think of the left side of the blackboard as the beginning and the right side of the blackboard as the end; the top of the blackboard as high and the bottom of the blackboard as low and make a mark that represents your sound." As Colgrass points out, "Anybody can make a mark on a blackboard." The marks may be squiggles, circles, dots or any combination that "looks like" their sound. When the person has done that, Colgrass would ask him or her to "sing" the mark so the group could learn it.

这还与普利策奖得主、古典音乐作曲家迈克尔·科尔格拉斯（Michael Colgrass）所发展出的一种方法惊人地相似——他能在四十五分钟内教各年龄段的人共同创作并记下一段合奏曲（见《天才的策略 第一卷》，pp. 251-256）。这方法让一群人"开始随便发出各种声音"。接着科尔格拉斯会让其中一人走到黑板前，"把黑板左边想成开头、右边想成结尾；黑板上方想成高、下方想成低，然后画一个代表你声音的记号。"正如科尔格拉斯指出的："任何人都能在黑板上画个记号。"那记号可以是涂鸦、圆圈、点，或任何"看起来像"其声音的任意组合。当那人画好，科尔格拉斯会请他/她把那记号"唱"出来，好让全组人学会。

He would then ask another person to come up and "make a mark" representing their sound. As they did, Colgrass would point out, "You already have one sound there and the second sound you're going to make has got to be in relation to that sound, but independent of it. It's got to come before it or over it or below it or after it. It may be quite different from it, or extremely similar to it."

接着他会请另一个人上前，"画一个记号"代表自己的声音。当他们这么做时，科尔格拉斯会指出："你那儿已经有一个声音了，而你要发出的第二个声音，必须与那声音相关、却又独立于它。它得在它之前、之上、之下、或之后。它可能与前者截然不同，也可能极为相似。"

Colgrass would continue in this way until the blackboard was full of "marks" contributed by the group that everyone knew how to "sing." He would then have someone "conduct" a performance of their collective "composition." For example, someone might say "I'm going to run my finger across the board. Wherever my finger touches, everybody just sing what you see." Others might be more directive, having different people or different groups "singing" various parts of the marks on the blackboard.

科尔格拉斯会如此继续，直到黑板上满是全组人贡献的、人人都会"唱"的"记号"。接着他会请某人来"指挥"他们集体"作品"的演出。例如，有人可能说"我要用手指划过黑板。手指碰到哪，大家就唱出你看到的东西。"也有人会更主导些，让不同的人或不同的组"唱出"黑板上记号的不同部分。

When they finished, as Colgrass points out, "they've heard sounds, put those sounds together in a composition by creating their own notation for it, conducted it, and performed it. In that one period of forty or fifty minutes, the past thousand years of music history has been re-created for them."

当他们结束时，正如科尔格拉斯所指出的："他们听到了声音，通过创造自己的记谱法把这些声音组合成一首曲子，指挥了它，并演出它。在那四五十分钟里，过去一千年的音乐史为他们重演了一遍。"

练习艾瑞克森转化阻抗的策略Practicing Erickson's Strategy to Transform Resistance

You can try this combination of patterns yourself by going through the following steps.

你可以通过以下步骤，亲自尝试这一模式组合。

  1. Pick a task that you believe is difficult or challenging for you but that you really want to learn or master (e.g., play a musical instrument, compose a song, write a book, establish a healthy habit, learn a foreign language, etc.).
  1. 选一件你认为对自己困难或有挑战、却真心想学习或掌握的任务（例如：演奏一种乐器、作一首曲、写一本书、建立一种健康习惯、学一门外语等）。
  2. 把任务向下归类成足够小的步骤，使你有信心能轻松独自完成其中任一步。
  3. 为你已经掌握、且觉得容易的其他任务创造一个类比（例如园艺、烹饪、桌游、电子游戏、运动等）。你所辨别的步骤，与此任务的步骤之间有何相似之处？
  4. 开始向上归类：把几个小步骤组合起来，练习/落实这组合，直到你有信心能轻松完成这组合。
  1. Chunk the task down into small enough steps such that you feel confident that you could easily do any one of the individual steps by itself.
  1. Create an analogy to some other tasks that you already have mastered and find easy for you (e.g., gardening, cooking, board game, video game, sport, etc.). What would be the parallels between the steps you have identified and the steps of this task?
  1. Begin to chunk up by combining a few small steps and practice/implement the combination until you are confident you can do that combination easily.

结语：米尔顿·艾瑞克森，与高创造力者的三项根本特质Conclusion: Milton Erickson and the Three Fundamental Attributes of Highly Creative People

In the introduction to this book, I outlined ten Patterns of Genius shared by the remarkable individuals I have examined in the previous volumes of this work. As we have seen, Milton Erickson's brilliant interventions clearly incorporate these macro strategies, along with his meta strategies of pacing, leading and utilization, and the micro strategies characterized by the Sleight of Mouth Patterns.

在本书的导论中，我勾勒了十项天才的模式，它们为我在本作前几卷中所研究的那些卓越人物所共享。如我们所见，米尔顿·艾瑞克森精妙的介入，明确纳入了这些宏观策略，连同他"先跟后带、利用"的元策略，以及以语言的魔力模式为特征的微观策略。

At the University of California at Santa Cruz, where NLP was initially developed, there was a psychology professor named Frank Baron. Baron spent his career studying highly creative people from many areas, including writers, architects, research scientists and mathematicians. As a result of these studies Barron identified a number of key characteristics of highly creative individuals. Ultimately, he synthesized what he had learned into three fundamental attributes.

在 NLP 最初发展的加州大学圣克鲁兹分校，曾有一位名叫弗兰克·巴伦（Frank Barron）的心理学教授。巴伦毕生研究来自许多领域的高创造力者，包括作家、建筑师、研究科学家和数学家。通过这些研究，巴伦辨识出高创造力个体的若干关键特征。最终，他将所学综合为三项根本特质。

Highly creative people are:

高创造力者是：

  1. comfortable with uncertainty
  2. able to hold seeming opposites or paradoxes
  3. persistent
  1. 安于不确定（comfortable with uncertainty）
  2. 能够容纳看似对立之物或悖论（able to hold seeming opposites or paradoxes）
  3. 坚持不懈（persistent）

Milton Erickson presents a clear and powerful of example of these attributes. Creative people, like Erickson, do not need to know the answer ahead of time. Not only are they able to tolerate uncertainty, but they also even enjoy not knowing. This state of "not knowing" or "beginner's mind" combined with curiosity is the essence of truly generative and transformational change.

米尔顿·艾瑞克森清晰地、有力地示范了这些特质。像艾瑞克森这样的创意者，无需预先知道答案。他们不仅能容忍不确定，甚至享受"不知"。这种"不知"或"初心"的状态，加上好奇，才是真正生成式与转化式变革的精髓。

Creative people can also hold differing viewpoints and multiple realities. The great Danish physicist Nils Bohr pointed out that there are two types of truth: superficial truth and deep truth. According to Bohr, "In a superficial truth, the opposite is false. In a deep truth, the opposite is also true." Bohr was referring to the fact that the most fundamental units of physical reality, such as photons and electrons, present a paradox. At times they behave like waves of energy and other times they behave like tiny particles of matter.

创意者也能容纳相异的观点与多重现实。伟大的丹麦物理学家尼尔斯·玻尔（Nils Bohr）指出，真理有两种：表层的真理与深层的真理。按玻尔的说法："在表层的真理中，其反面是假；在深层的真理中，其反面亦为真。"玻尔所指的，是物理现实最基础的单元（如光子与电子）呈现出一种悖论：它们时而表现得像能量波，时而表现得像微小的物质粒子。

Such deep truths are also at the foundation of our subjective experience. The fact that we can experience someone as beautiful does not mean that they cannot also be simultaneously ugly. Joy does not come without sadness. The worst thing that has ever happened to you can also be the best thing that has ever happened to you. Where there is light, there are also shadows.

这类深层真理，也立于我们主观经验的根基之上。我们能体验到某人是美的，并不意味他不能同时是丑的。喜悦不会没有悲伤而来。曾发生在你身上最糟的事，也可能正是曾发生在你身上最好的事。有光之处，亦有影。

The capacity to be aware of these seeming opposite realities without one having to be "right" and the other "wrong" is an essential aspect of generativity. Gregory Bateson[^1] maintained, "Wisdom comes from sitting together and truthfully confronting our differences, without the intention to change anything." When we can hold different perspectives with curiosity, new and surprising solutions often emerge.

能够觉知这些看似对立的现实，而不必一方"对"、另一方"错"，是生成力的一个重要面向。格里高利·贝特森（Gregory Bateson）[^1] 主张："智慧，来自并肩而坐、如实面对彼此的差异，且无意改变任何东西。"当我们能以好奇容纳不同视角，崭新而意外的解决方案常会涌现。

Erickson was as master at welcoming and holding paradox. In his words:

艾瑞克森是欢迎并容纳悖论的个中大师。用他自己的话来说：

It is important to have a sense of security; a sense of readiness; a full knowledge that, come what may, you can meet it and handle it — and enjoy doing it. It's also nice learning to come up against the situation that you can't handle—and then later think it over and realize that, too, was a
拥有一种安全感很重要；一种准备就绪的感觉；一种全然的知晓：无论如何，你都能迎上它、处理它——并乐在其中。学会撞上你处理不了的境况，也挺好——然后事后思量，领悟到那也是一种

learning that is useful in many, many different ways. It allows you to assess your strength. It also allows you to discover the areas in which you need to use some more of your own security, which rests within yourself...

在许多许多方面都有用的学习。它让你能估量自己的力量。它也让你发现，哪些领域你需要更多地运用你自身内在的安全感……

Reacting to the good and the bad, and dealing with it adequately— that's the real joy in life. (Patterns of the Hypnotic Techniques of Milton H. Erickson, M.D. Volume II, pp. 210-212)
对好与坏都做出反应，并恰当地应对——那才是生命真正的喜悦。（《米尔顿·H·艾瑞克森医学博士催眠技术模式 第二卷》，pp. 210-212）

This is where the quality of persistence is also important. Highly creative people don't give up, even in the face of uncertainty and dilemma. They remain curious to discover what is possible and continue searching.

这也正是坚持不懈这一特质重要的所在。高创造力者不放弃，即便面对不确定与两难。他们始终好奇于去发现什么是可能的，并持续探寻。

Milton Erickson epitomized this quality in many ways. It was a quality he demonstrated throughout his life. When he was 17 years old, for example, he was stricken with a severe case of polio. The illness reached the point where he was unable to move at all. At one point, he overheard the doctors telling his mother that he would probably never speak or walk again; and essentially be a vegetable. Later on, Erickson heard them tell his mother that he probably wouldn't even survive until the morning. Naturally, this was quite upsetting to her. According to Erickson, he thought that was a terrible thing for anybody to say to a mother, so he began an intense journey to discover what was possible.

米尔顿·艾瑞克森在许多方面都是这一特质的典范。这是他一生都在展现的特质。例如，他十七岁时染上严重的脊髓灰质炎。病情发展到他完全无法动弹。有一回，他无意中听到医生对他母亲说，他大概再也不能说话或走路，基本上成了植物人。后来，艾瑞克森又听到他们对母亲说，他恐怕连那个早晨都活不过去。这自然令她极为难过。据艾瑞克森说，他觉得对一位母亲说出这种话实在恶劣，于是他开始了一段全力以赴的旅程，去发现什么是可能的。

Erickson spent hours trying to see if there was any part of his body that he could move. He finally discovered that he could slightly control one of his eyelids. And so for the next several hours when his mother would come by, he would try to move his eyelid to get her attention. Once he had managed this, for several more hours he struggled to work out a signal system with her. After much time and effort, he finally succeeded in communicating to her the thing that he was so determined to get across. He wanted her to turn his bed toward the window, so he could watch the sun come up the next day.

艾瑞克森花了数小时，试图看身体有没有哪个部分还能动。他终于发现，自己能略微控制一只眼皮。于是接下来的几个小时里，每当母亲经过，他便试着动眼皮来引起她的注意。一旦做到这点，他又花了好几个小时，努力与她建立一个信号系统。经过大量时间与努力，他终于成功地向她传达了那个他下定决心要表达的意思：他想让她把病床转向窗户，好让他次日能看着太阳升起。

She did and Erickson began a healing journey, defying all medical expectations, to recover his ability to speak and move his body. This undoubtedly involved chunking every movement down to its minute components and deliberately practicing them until he had mastered them. Then, staying curious to discover what was possible, chunking these micro movements up into the key combinations and sequences that he would need in order to function in the world.

母亲照做了，艾瑞克森便开始了一段康复之旅，违逆所有医学预期，重新找回说话与活动身体的能力。这无疑涉及把每个动作向下归类到极细微的组成部分，并有意识地练习，直到掌握为止。接着，保持好奇去发现什么是可能的，再把这些微小动作向上归类为他在世上运作所需的关键组合与序列。

This was the same type of persistence, grounded in his own experience of self-determination, that Erickson brought into his work with his clients. He did not give up trying to find what was possible, no matter how challenging a situation seemed. And he did not assume anything was impossible.

这正是艾瑞克森带入其与案主工作中的那类坚持不懈——植根于他自身的自我决定经验。无论情境看似多具挑战，他都不放弃去寻找什么是可能的。他也从不预设任何事是不可能的。

As Erickson's life illustrates, this combination of beginner's mind (comfort with uncertainty), curiosity and persistence is at the core of the truly creative and innovative thinking that produces genius. We shall witness these same characteristics in the other creative geniuses we will be exploring in the coming chapters.

正如艾瑞克森的一生所示范，这种初心（安于不确定）、好奇与坚持不懈的组合，正是催生天才的那真正具创意与创新的思考之核心。在我们将于后续章节探索的其他创意天才身上，我们也将见证这些同样的特质。

They were interested in getting background information on hypnosis and trance. Chapter 2: Gregory Bateson — 格里高里·贝特森 — 连接之模式Chapter 2: Gregory Bateson — The Patterns Which Connect

〔图 p146〕Bateson 头像铅笔素描图

章节概览Chapter Overview

In this chapter, I invite you to step into the extraordinary mind of Gregory Bateson—one of the most influential thinkers behind the epistemological foundations of NLP and what I have come to call Generative Intelligence. While Milton Erickson showed us how to facilitate change, Bateson helps us understand the larger ecology within which change occurs. His genius lay in perceiving the deeper patterns connecting mind, nature, communication and systems into a unified whole.

在本章中，我邀请你走进格里高里·贝特森（Gregory Bateson）这位非凡心智的世界——他是 NLP 认识论基础以及我称之为「生生不息的智慧」（Generative Intelligence）的思想背后最具影响力的思想家之一。如果说米尔顿·艾瑞克森向我们展示了如何促成改变，那么贝特森则帮助我们理解改变得以发生的更宏大的生态。他的天才在于洞察到将心智、自然、沟通与系统连接成一个统一整体的那些更深层模式。

As you move through this chapter, you will begin to recognize that many distinctions central to NLP—logical levels, meta position, feedback versus failure, multiple perspectives and systemic thinking—are deeply rooted in Bateson's work.

随着你读进本章，你会开始意识到，NLP 中许多核心的区分——逻辑层次、元位置、反馈而非失败、多重观点与系统思维——都深深植根于贝特森的工作。

连接之模式：系统性地思考The Pattern Which Connects: Thinking Systemically

At the heart of Bateson's genius was his search for what he called "the pattern which connects." Rather than focusing on isolated elements, he explored the relationships, feedback loops and structures that generate meaning and behavior. This shift from content to pattern is one of the chapter's most important distinctions. You will discover that systems are defined more by relationships than by parts, and that change emerges through interaction rather than simple linear cause and effect.

贝特森天才的核心，是他对于自己所谓「连接之模式」（the pattern which connects）的不断探寻。他关注的不是孤立的元素，而是生成意义与行为的那些关系、反馈回路与结构。这种从「内容」到「模式」的转向，是本章最重要的区分之一。你会发现，系统更多是由关系而非部件所定义，而改变是通过互动涌现的，而非简单的线性因果。

逻辑类型与逻辑层次：改变的层级Logical Types and Logical Levels: Hierarchies of Change

A major theme throughout the chapter is Bateson's exploration of logical levels and logical types. He recognized that many forms of confusion, paradox and pathology arise when different levels of process become mixed together. These insights became foundational in my own development of Neuro-Logical Levels and continue to provide one of the clearest ways to understand how change operates within complex human systems.

贯穿本章的一个主题是贝特森对逻辑层次（logical levels）与逻辑类型（logical types）的探索。他认识到，许多混淆、悖论与病态，都源于不同层次的过程被混在了一起。这些洞见成了我自己发展「神经逻辑层次」（Neuro-Logical Levels）的基础，并且至今仍是理解改变如何在复杂人类系统中运作的最清晰途径之一。

溯因思维：隐喻的力量Abductive Thinking: The Power of Metaphor

Bateson also emphasized that we do not experience reality directly—we experience maps of reality. From this perspective, transformation often comes not from changing reality itself, but from enriching the maps through which reality is perceived. This naturally leads to one of Bateson's most creative strategies: abductive thinking, or the ability to recognize similar patterns across very different domains through metaphor and analogy. Rather than relying solely on linear logic, Bateson moved fluidly between intuition and rigor, imagery and structure, metaphor and precision. This movement between different forms of knowing lies at the heart of creativity and generative problem solving.

贝特森还强调，我们并非直接经验现实——我们经验的是现实的地图。从这一视角看，转化往往并非来自改变现实本身，而是来自丰富用以感知现实的那些地图。这自然引出了贝特森最具创造力的策略之一：溯因思维（abductive thinking），即通过隐喻与类比，辨认出截然不同领域之间相似模式的能力。贝特森不依赖单纯的线性逻辑，而是在直觉与严谨、意象与结构、隐喻与精确之间灵活移动。这种在不同认知形式之间的游走，正是创造力与生成式问题解决的心脏。

控制论与反馈：作为系统的学习Cybernetics and Feedback: Learning as a System

Another key contribution explored in this chapter is Bateson's work in cybernetics and feedback. He reframed learning and change as self-organizing processes regulated through feedback loops rather than

本章探讨的另一项关键贡献，是贝特森在控制论（cybernetics）与反馈（feedback）方面的工作。他将学习与改变重新框定为通过反馈回路自我调节的过程，而非由外部强加的控制。从这一视角看，我们通常所谓的「错误」变成了学习与适应的信号，而非失败。

externally imposed control. From this perspective, what we typically call "errors" become signals for learning and adaptation rather than failures.

裂化生成与双重束缚Schismogenesis and Double Binds

You will also encounter Bateson's powerful models of schismogenesis and the double bind. Schismogenesis describes escalating relational patterns—such as conflict cycles or dominance/submission dynamics—that can destabilize systems if left unchecked. Double binds occur when contradictory messages are communicated at different logical levels, creating "no-win" situations that generate chronic confusion and stress. Bateson demonstrated that these patterns operate not only in clinical settings, but in families, organizations and even in our own internal dialogue.

你还会遇到贝特森强有力的模型：裂化生成（schismogenesis）与双重束缚（double bind）。裂化生成描述的是不断升级的关系模式——例如冲突循环或支配/顺从的动力学——若不加以抑制，便会瓦解系统。双重束缚则发生在不同逻辑层次上传达出相互矛盾的信息时，制造出导致长期混淆与压力的「无解」处境。贝特森证明，这些模式不仅运作于临床情境，也运作于家庭、组织，甚至我们自身的内在对话之中。

元位置与元沟通Meta Position and Meta-Communication

A key pathway out of these systemic traps is the development of meta position—the capacity to step back and observe the system rather than become trapped within it. Bateson consistently emphasized the importance of communicating about communication itself and bringing awareness to the structure of relationships and interactions. This capacity for meta-awareness is one of the foundations of generative change.

走出这些系统性陷阱的一条关键路径，是发展出元位置（meta position）——退后一步、观察系统而非困陷其中的能力。贝特森一贯强调沟通「关于」沟通本身的重要性，并将觉察带入关系与互动的结构之中。这种元觉察的能力，是生成式改变的基础之一。

你可能遇到的挑战Challenges You May Encounter

As you engage with Bateson's work, you may notice that it can feel abstract, paradoxical and intellectually demanding. It often requires us to tolerate ambiguity, hold multiple perspectives simultaneously and remain open to "not knowing." Yet this is part of Bateson's strategy of genius. Rather than providing fixed answers, he invites us to develop more ecological and systemic ways of thinking. Bateson often suggested that many human problems arise because we are too certain of our answers and not curious enough about our questions.

当你深入贝特森的工作时，你或许会注意到它显得抽象、悖谬、在智识上极费力气。它常常要求我们容忍模糊、同时容纳多重观点，并对「不知道」保持开放。然而，这正是贝特森天才策略的一部分。他不是提供固定的答案，而是邀请我们发展出更具生态性与系统性的思维方式。贝特森常指出，许多人类问题之所以产生，是因为我们对答案太过确定，而对问题却不够好奇。

为何值得付出努力Why It Is Worth the Effort

The reward for staying with this material is profound. Bateson's work helps us move beyond simplistic cause-and-effect thinking into a richer understanding of the dynamic patterns that shape learning, communication, leadership and transformation. Most importantly, it helps us begin to perceive "the pattern which connects"—the deeper coherence linking seemingly unrelated aspects of experience.

坚持琢磨这些材料的回报是深远的。贝特森的工作帮助我们超越简化的因果思维，进入对塑造学习、沟通、领导与转化的动态模式更丰富的理解。最重要的是，它帮助我们开始觉察「连接之模式」——将经验中看似无关的各个面向联结起来的更深层 coherence（一致性）。

更深的邀请The Deeper Invitation

For me personally, studying with Bateson was one of the most transformative experiences of my life. His work continually challenged me to move beyond linear models into a more ecological understanding of mind and change.

就我个人而言，与贝特森共学是我生命中最具转化性的体验之一。他的工作不断挑战我，让我超越线性模型，进入对心智与改变更具生态性的理解。

This chapter is not simply about understanding Bateson's ideas. It is an invitation into a different epistemology—a different way of knowing. As you continue, I encourage you not to rush to "figure it out," but to allow the patterns to emerge gradually over time.

本章不仅仅是理解贝特森的想法。它是对一种不同认识论——一种不同认知方式——的邀请。我鼓励你继续往下时，不要急于「把它搞懂」，而是让这些模式随时间逐渐浮现。

Bateson reminds us that the deepest problems—and the deepest solutions—rarely exist in isolated individuals or events. They arise from the patterns of relationship within larger systems. Learning to perceive and engage those patterns is one of the foundations of generative intelligence.

贝特森提醒我们，最深层的问题——以及最深层的解法——很少存在于孤立的个人或事件中。它们源于更大系统之中关系的模式。学会觉察并介入这些模式，是生生不息的智慧的基础之一。

〔图 p144〕

One of the key principles of Gregory Bateson's work is to find "the pattern that connects"... what is the common denominator that at some level unites items that seem to be different?

格里高里·贝特森Gregory Bateson

Gregory Bateson (1904–1980) was an anthropologist and social scientist whose ideas and theories significantly influenced fields as varied as anthropology, linguistics, communication theory, cybernetics, psychiatry and systemic psychotherapy. In the field of anthropology, for example, Bateson studied Balinese culture with Margaret Mead (his first wife) and together they published a book on their observations that was the first to use photography to analyze cultural patterns. These studies led Bateson to formulate the notions of "complementary," "symmetrical," and "reciprocal" relationships, along with the notions of "schismogenesis" and "ethos" within a culture. His observations of non-Western cultures also led him to propose the idea of "kinesthetic" learning. In fact, it was his study of trance dancing in Bali that led Bateson to his first contact with Milton Erickson in the 1930s (through their mutual friend and renowned writer Aldous Huxley—author of Brave New World and The Doors of Perception).

格里高里·贝特森（1904–1980）是一位人类学家与社会科学家，其思想与理论深刻影响了人类学、语言学、沟通理论、控制论、精神医学与系统心理治疗等诸多领域。例如，在人类学领域，贝特森与玛格丽特·米德（Margaret Mead，他的第一任妻子）一同研究巴厘岛文化，两人合作出版的观察著作，是首部运用摄影来分析文化模式的书。这些研究使贝特森提炼出文化中的「互补」（complementary）、「对称」（symmetrical）与「互易」（reciprocal）关系，以及「裂化生成」与「精神特质」（ethos）等概念。他对非西方文化的观察，也让他提出了「动觉」（kinesthetic）学习的理念。事实上，正是他在 1930 年代对巴厘岛催眠舞蹈（trance dancing）的研究，让他通过共同的朋友、著名作家奥尔德斯·赫胥黎（《美丽新世界》与《感知之门》的作者）首次接触到了米尔顿·艾瑞克森。

As a systems theorist Bateson participated in the famous Macy conferences (in 1949) that gave birth to the field of cybernetics. In the areas of animal behavior and communications theory he drew from the philosophical work of Bertrand Russell and Alfred North Whitehead to identify different logical types and logical levels of communication and learning that explained many of the problems and anomalies in behaviorism.

作为一位系统理论家，贝特森参与了 1949 年著名的梅西会议（Macy conferences）——正是它催生了控制论这一领域。在动物行为学与沟通理论领域，他借鉴了伯特兰·罗素与阿尔弗雷德·诺斯·怀特海的哲学著作，辨识出不同的逻辑类型与逻辑层次的沟通与学习，从而解释了行为主义中许多的问题与异常。

In the areas of psychiatry and psychology Bateson formulated the "double bind" theory of schizophrenia and pioneered the process of systemic family therapy. His ideas, for instance, were used by Mara Selvini Palazzoli as the foundation for the Milan School of Systemic Therapy. In 1959, Bateson joined with Don Jackson, Jules Ruskin and Virginia Satir to start the prestigious Mental Research Institute in Palo Alto, California. Together, they created the country's first formal program in Family Therapy.

在精神医学与心理学领域，贝特森提出了精神分裂症的「双重束缚」理论，并开创了系统家庭治疗（systemic family therapy）的进程。例如，玛拉·塞尔维尼·帕拉佐利（Mara Selvini Palazzoli）便以他的思想为米兰系统治疗学派（Milan School of Systemic Therapy）的基础。1959 年，贝特森与唐·杰克逊（Don Jackson）、朱尔斯·拉斯金（Jules Ruskin）和维吉尼亚·萨提亚（Virginia Satir）联手，在加州帕罗奥图的创立了声望卓著的心理研究所（Mental Research Institute）。他们一起创建了全美首个正式的家庭治疗项目。

Bateson's research also included the study of play in animals, and of communication between dolphins and porpoises.

贝特森的研究还包括对动物游戏行为，以及海豚与鼠海豚之间沟通的研究。

Bateson had an incredible ability to recognize order and pattern in the universe. He is an iconic example of the pattern of genius involving thinking systemically and focusing on the "deep structure" beyond the "surface structure." He emphasized that it was more important to observe the interactions and relationships between the elements in a system, rather than the particular elements themselves. Bateson's emphasis on "levels of learning," the need for a more developed connection between "mind" and "body," ecology and the aesthetics of change are examples of this shift of focus.

贝特森拥有一种在宇宙中辨认秩序与模式的惊人能力。他是「系统性地思考」并聚焦于「表层结构」背后的「深层结构」这一天才模式的标志性范例。他强调，观察系统中各元素之间的互动与关系，比观察特定元素本身更为重要。贝特森对「学习的层次」、对「心智」与「身体」之间更发达联结的需求、生态学以及改变的审美，都是这种焦点转移的例子。

格里高里·贝特森与 NLP 的联结The Connection of Gregory Bateson to NLP

While his influence often goes unrecognized, Gregory Bateson also made many fundamental contributions to the field of NLP. As I mentioned in the previous chapter on Milton Erickson, in the Fall of 1975 Grinder and Bandler showed Bateson the manuscript for their book The Structure of Magic which outlined the language patterns known in NLP as the "Meta Model." Bateson was impressed with the work, and wrote in his preface:

尽管他的影响常常未被承认，格里高里·贝特森对 NLP 领域也作出了许多基础性贡献。正如我在前一章关于米尔顿·艾瑞克森时所提到的，1975 年秋，格林德（Grinder）与班德勒（Bandler）向贝特森展示了他们著作《魔法的结构》（The Structure of Magic）的手稿，其中勾勒了 NLP 中被称为「元模型」（Meta Model）的语言模式。贝特森对这项工作印象深刻，并在序言中写道：

John Grinder and Richard Bandler have done something similar to what my colleagues and I attempted fifteen years ago... They have tools which we did not have—or did not see how to use. They have succeeded in making linguistics into a base for theory and simultaneously into a tool for therapy... making explicit the syntax of how people avoid change, and, therefore, how to assist them in changing.
约翰·格林德与理查德·班德勒所做的事，与我及同事们十五年前试图做的事相似……他们拥有我们当时没有、或不知如何使用的工具。他们成功地将语言学变成了理论的基础，同时也变成了治疗的一种工具……把人们如何回避改变、以及如何协助他们改变的句法变得显明。

Bateson's earlier work, to which he referred in his preface, was his attempt to apply principles of cybernetics and communication theory to psychotherapy and the understanding of psychological pathology. Stimulated by Norbert Wiener (the founder of cybernetics), Bateson had adapted cybernetic thinking to human communication and interaction in order to develop generalizations about the behavior and mental characteristics of individuals, groups and families, and the influences that produced either functional or dysfunctional systems. As I mentioned above, these ideas fueled a whole generation of behavioral scientists and psychotherapists.

贝特森在序言中所指的更早的工作，是他尝试将控制论与沟通理论的原则应用于心理治疗，以及理解心理病理学。受控制论创始人诺伯特·维纳（Norbert Wiener）的激发，贝特森将控制论思维调适到了人类沟通与互动之中，以发展出关于个人、群体与家庭的行为与心智特征，以及造成功能良好或功能失调系统的那些影响的概括性认识。正如我上面提到的，这些思想推动了一整代行为科学家与心理治疗师。

In the early days of NLP, Bandler, Grinder and Bateson all lived on the same property at 1000 Alba Road in Ben Lomond, California. The property was owned by Robert Spitzer, a wealthy benefactor who supported developments in the human potential movement. Spitzer also owned Science and Behavior Books, which published works by Fritz Perls and Virginia Satir as well as Bandler and Grinder's first books. Spitzer's land had a number of houses and cottages on it, and he let people that he felt were making a difference live on this property very inexpensively. As neighbors, Bateson, Bandler and Grinder had numerous interactions and conversations; frequently about fundamental patterns and principles relating to life, mind, change and choice.

在 NLP 早期，班德勒、格林德与贝特森都住在加州本·洛蒙德（Ben Lomond）阿尔巴路 1000 号（1000 Alba Road）的同一处房产里。这处房产属于罗伯特·斯皮策（Robert Spitzer）——一位支持人类潜能运动的富有赞助人。斯皮策还拥有科学和行为图书（Science and Behavior Books）出版社，该社出版了弗里茨·皮尔斯（Fritz Perls）与维吉尼亚·萨提亚，以及班德勒与格林德早期著作。斯皮策的土地上建有多栋房屋与村舍，他让那些他认为正在创造价值的人以极低的租金住在这里。作为邻居，贝特森、班德勒与格林德有大量的互动与交谈；话题常常关乎生命、心智、改变与选择的基本模式与原则。

Consequently, Bateson's thoughts and ideas were a major influence in shaping the epistemological foundations of NLP. In fact, it was Bateson's work in communications theory that provided the theoretical underpinnings for much of NLP as a discipline. The NLP notions of "meta position," "meta communication," "meta messages," and the detection of verbal and non-verbal incongruence, for instance, were derived directly from Bateson's work.

由此，贝特森的思想与理念对塑造 NLP 的认识论基础产生了重大影响。事实上，正是贝特森在沟通理论上的工作，为作为一门学科的 NLP 提供了大量的理论支撑。例如，NLP 中的「元位置」「元沟通」（meta communication）、「元信息」（meta messages），以及对言语与非言语不一致性的侦测，都直接源于贝特森的工作。

The fundamental tenets of NLP New Code, encapsulated in the book Turtles All The Way Down (DeLozier and Grinder, 1987), draw heavily from Bateson's work in the area of systems theory. Key NLP concepts, such as "states," "metaphor," "conscious/unconscious relationships," "perceptual positions," "multiple descriptions," "perceptual filters," and "levels of learning and change," for instance, were also directly inspired by Bateson's ideas.

收录于《乌龟一路向下》（Turtles All The Way Down，DeLozier 与 Grinder，1987）一书中的 NLP 新密码（New Code）的基本原则，大量汲取了贝特森在系统理论领域的工作。关键的 NLP 概念，如「状态」（states）、「隐喻」、「意识/潜意识关系」、「知觉位置」（perceptual positions）、「多重描述」（multiple descriptions）、「知觉滤镜」（perceptual filters）以及「学习与改变的层次」，也都直接受到贝特森思想的启发。

In addition to the NLP principles and patterns mentioned above many of the so-called "Presuppositions of NLP" were inspired by Bateson's deeply systemic perspective.

除了上述 NLP 原则与模式之外，许多所谓的「NLP 预设」（Presuppositions of NLP），也都受到贝特森深刻的系统性视角的启发。

贝特森对 NLP 预设的影响Bateson's Influence on the Presuppositions of NLP

The following groupings of NLP presuppositions were either directly or indirectly influenced by Bateson and, as we shall see in the coming pages, reflect much about his meta strategies of genius.

以下这些 NLP 预设的分组，或是直接、或是间接受到了贝特森的影响；并且，正如我们在接下来的篇幅中将会看到的，它们折射出他的天才元策略的诸多面向。

一、地图不是疆域I. The Map is not the Territory

  1. People respond to their own perceptions of reality.
  1. 人们对自身对现实的感知作出反应。
  2. 每个人都有自己个人的世界观地图。每张地图都有其有效性，但没有哪张个人地图比任何其他地图更「真实」或更「正确」。
  3. 最「明智」且最「慈悲」的地图，是那些整合了多重观点、并提供最宽广与最丰富的选择数量的地图。
  4. 人会在其所感知到的、从其世界观模型中可得的可能性与能力范围内，做出当下可得的最佳选择。任何行为，无论它看起来多么邪恶、疯狂或怪异，对当事人而言都是当下可得的最佳选择——如果能给出（在其世界观模型之情境中的）更恰当的选择，当事人便更可能去采取它。
  5. 人已经拥有（或潜在地拥有）有效行动所需的全部资源。
  6. 改变来自于释放适当的资源，或通过丰富一个人的世界观地图，为特定情境激活潜在资源。
  1. Every person has their own individual map of the world. Every map has some validity, but no individual map of the world is any more "real" or "true" than any other.
  1. The "wisest" and most "compassionate" maps are those that integrate multiple perspectives and make available the widest and richest number of choices.
  1. People make the best choices available to them given possibilities and the capabilities that they perceive available to them from their model of the world. Any behavior no matter how evil, crazy or bizarre it seems is the best choice available to the person at that point in time – if given a more appropriate choice (within the context of their model of the world) the person will be more likely to take it.
  1. People already have (or potentially have) all of the resources they need to act effectively.
  1. Change comes from releasing the appropriate resource, or activating the potential resource, for a particular context by enriching a person's map of the world.

二、生命与「心智」是系统性的过程II. Life And "Mind" Are Systemic Processes

  1. The processes that take place within a person, and between people and their environment, are systemic. Our bodies, our societies and our universe form an ecology of systems and sub-systems (holons) all of which interact with and mutually influence each other.
  1. 发生于一个人内部、以及人与其环境之间的过程，都是系统性的。我们的身体、我们的社会、我们的宇宙，构成了一个系统与共系统（全息子，holons）的生态，它们全都彼此互动、相互影响着。
  2. 不可能将系统的任何部分与系统的其余部分完全隔离。人不可能不影响彼此。人与人之间的互动形成反馈回路——如此，一个人会受到自己行为对他人所造成的结果的影响。
  3. 系统是「自组织」的，并自然地寻求平衡与稳定状态。没有失败，只有反馈。
  4. 任何反应、经验或行为，若脱离其被建立的情境或它接下来所引发的反应，便毫无意义。任何行为、经验或反应，都可能是资源，也可能是限制——端看它如何与系统的其余部分相契合。
  5. 并非系统中的所有互动都处于同一层次。在某一层次上为正面的，在另一层次上可能为负面的。将行为与「身份」分离是有用的——将产生该行为的正面意图、功能、信念等，与行为本身分离开来。
  6. 在某种程度上，所有行为都（或曾经）是「正面意图的」。从行为所属之人的角度看，给定行为被建立于其中的情境，它当时被感知为恰当的。回应意图，而非回应问题行为的表达，要更容易、也更有成效。
  7. 环境与情境会改变。同样的行动不会总是产生同样的结果。为了成功适应与生存，系统的一个成员需要某种最低限度的灵活性。这种灵活性的程度，必须与系统其余部分的变异程度成比例。随着系统变得愈发复杂，所需的灵活性也越多。
  1. It is not possible to completely isolate any part of a system from the rest of the system. People cannot not influence each other. Interactions between people form feedback loops – such that a person will be affected by the results that their own actions make on other people.

在本章余下的部分，贝特森的天才如何成为形塑这些预设的关键影响，将变得显而易见。

  1. Systems are "self-organizing" and naturally seek states of balance and stability. There are no failures, only feedback.
  1. No response, experience or behavior is meaningful outside of the context in which it was established or the response it elicits next. Any behavior, experience or response may serve as a resource or limitation depending on how it fits in with the rest of the system.
  1. Not all interactions in a system are on the same level. What is positive on one level may be negative on another level. It is useful to separate behavior from "identity" – to separate the positive intent, function, belief, etc. that generates the behavior from the behavior itself.
  1. At some level all behavior is (or at one time was) "positively intended." It is or was perceived as appropriate given the context in which it was established, from the point of view of the person whose behavior it is. It is easier and more productive to respond to the intention rather than the expression of a problematic behavior.
  1. Environments and contexts change. The same action will not always produce the same result. In order to successfully adapt and survive, a member of a system needs a certain minimum amount of flexibility. That amount of flexibility has to be proportional to the variation in the rest of the system. As a system becomes more complex, more flexibility is required.

In the remainder of this chapter, it will become evident how Bateson's genius was a key influence on the formulation of these presuppositions.

我与格里高里·贝特森的个人经历My Personal Experience with Gregory Bateson

My own history with Bateson began in the Fall of 1975. John Grinder was intrigued by my application of his and Bandler's Meta Model language patterns to the Socratic dialogues in Plato's Republic (mentioned in the previous chapter) and suggested that I make a similar analysis of Gregory Bateson's "metalogues" from his book Steps to an Ecology of Mind. Bateson chose the term "metalogue," as a clear reference to the Socratic dialogues, to describe his own conversational method of "thinking about thinking" and challenging assumptions.

我与贝特森的渊源始于 1975 年秋。约翰·格林德对我将他与班德勒的元模型语言模式应用于柏拉图《理想国》（Plato's Republic）中的苏格拉底对话（前一章提到过）颇感兴味，便建议我对格里高里·贝特森《走向心智生态学》（Steps to an Ecology of Mind）一书中的「元对话」（metalogues）做类似的分析。贝特森选用「元对话」一词，正是清楚地指向苏格拉底对话，用以描述他自己的「对思考进行思考」、挑战假设的交谈方法。

The hypothesis was that Bateson's Metalogues intuitively applied particular Meta Model language patterns to systematically address other types of verbal categories in addition to those in Socrates' Dialogues—in particular nominalizations, which are words (such as "communication," "thought," "conflict," "freedom," etc.) that turn actions, ongoing processes and relationships into "things." This intuition proved to be correct, and I found myself deeply invigorated by Bateson's way of approaching his subjects.

当时的假说是：贝特森的元对话，直觉性地应用了特定的元模型语言模式，系统性地处理了除苏格拉底对话之外的其他类型的言语范畴——尤其是名词化（nominalizations），即那些将行动、进行中的过程与关系变成「事物」的词（如「沟通」「思想」「冲突」「自由」等）。这一直觉被证明是正确的，而我发现自己被贝特森接近其主题的方式深深激活了。

A year later, in the Fall of 1976 I attended Gregory Bateson's Ecology of Mind class at Kresge College at UC Santa Cruz. (Bateson had been appointed to the Board of Regents of the University of California earlier that year). I can say unequivocally that Bateson had the greatest depth and scope of thought of anyone I have ever known. His lectures would cover topics ranging from communication theory to Balinese art, to Maxwell's equations for electromagnetic fields, to schizophrenia, to genetic deformities in beetles' legs. His talks, however, were never a disjointed collection of thoughts or jumbled group of ideas as the diversity of subjects might suggest. Bateson's version of cybernetics and systems theory was able to tap into the deeper structure, or "pattern which connects," all of these topics into a single fascinating weave of life and existence.

一年后，1976 年秋，我在加州大学圣克鲁兹分校（UC Santa Cruz）的克瑞斯吉学院（Kresge College）旁听了格里高里·贝特森的心智生态学课程。（那一年早些时候，贝特森获任命为加州大学董事会成员）。我可以明确地说，贝特森是我所认识的人中，思想深度与广度最大的一位。他的讲座涵盖的主题，从沟通理论到巴厘岛艺术，到电磁场的麦克斯韦方程组，到精神分裂症，到甲虫腿部的基因畸形。然而，正如主题的多样性所可能暗示的，他的演讲绝非一堆散乱的思想或混杂的观念拼凑。贝特森版的控制论与系统理论，能够触及深层结构，或「连接之模式」，将以上所有主题编织成一幅关于生命与存在的单一而迷人的织锦。

Reflecting back, attending Bateson's course was one of the most transformative experiences of my life. I would sit in his class, listening to his deep voice and distinctive Cambridge accent, which sounded to me like the voice of universal wisdom. For me, he was, and remains, a type of "spiritual guide." Thoughts, ideas and revelations would flow into my mind, some relating to the contents of his lecture and some from completely other areas of my life, education, and experience. Usually, they came so quickly I couldn't write them down fast enough.

回想起来，旁听贝特森的课是我生命中最具转化性的体验之一。我会坐在他的课堂上，聆听他低沉的嗓音与独特的剑桥口音——那声音在我听来犹如 universal wisdom（普遍智慧）之声。对我而言，他过去是、现在仍是一种类型的「灵性向导」。思想、观念与启示会流入我的脑海，有些与他的讲座内容相关，有些则来自我生命、教育与经验中全然不同的领域。通常它们来得如此之快，我来不及把它们记下来。

My second class involving Bateson was a student directed seminar organized by a classmate Charles Sargenti and sponsored by Bateson entitled Studies in Schizophrenia. Typical of the emphasis on experiential leaning that characterized that period, one of the requirements of the class was to spend at least six hours a week with individuals who had been diagnosed as psychotic or schizophrenic. I volunteered at a local locked-ward private psychiatric facility that provided only "chemotherapy" for their patients. I also volunteered at a place called the "Soteria House" that had the opposite

我涉及贝特森的第二门课，是由同学查尔斯·萨尔詹蒂（Charles Sargenti）组织、由贝特森赞助的学生主导研讨班，名为精神分裂症研究。作为那个时期典型的经验式学习取向，这门课的要求之一，是每周至少花六小时与那些被诊断为精神错乱或精神分裂的人在一起。我自愿去了一家当地上锁病房的精神病私立机构，那里只用「化疗」对待病人。我也自愿去了一个叫「索特里亚之家」（Soteria House）的地方，那里的理念正相反——

philosophy and offered no drug treatment for patients but only emotional support by untrained volunteers.

不为病人提供任何药物治疗，只由未经训练的同愿者提供情感支持。

Bateson had proposed that many symptoms of "psychosis" were more than simply the result of individual genetic and chemical disturbances. He viewed some symptoms as a response to pathologies in the larger family and social systems in which they occurred. He also knew, as an anthropologist, that behaviors that were considered problematic, odd or "crazy" in one culture were tolerated, accepted and even encouraged in others. As I interacted with the various patients in both institutions in which I was volunteering, many of the stigmas and myths of psychosis disappeared and the significance of Bateson's view became clear. The importance of viewing the "identified patient" or problem within the context of the larger ecosystem surrounding them is a learning that I have taken with me the rest of my life.

贝特森曾提出，许多「精神错乱」的症状，不仅仅是个体遗传与化学紊乱的结果。他将某些症状视为对它们所发生的更大之家庭与社会系统之病态的反应。作为人类学家，他也知道，在一种文化中被认为有问题、古怪或「疯癫」的行为，在另一种文化中却被容忍、接纳、甚至被鼓励。当我在两家机构中与各色病人互动时，许多关于精神错乱的污名与迷思消散了，贝特森观点的意义也变得清晰。将「被指认的病人」或问题，置于环绕他们的更大生态系统之情境中来审视，是我此后一生都随身携带的一课。

The importance of the relationship between the individual and the larger system became frightfully clear to me one day when I was getting ready to leave the private psychiatric facility. I arrived at the locked door and said I was ready to go home. The staff at the door had changed shifts while I had been there, however, and no one recognized me. As a volunteer, I had no official badge or documentation. They thought I was a mental patient and were not going to let me out. When I tried to explain that I was a volunteer, they just nodded, saying, "Sure, sure, of course you are." Nothing I said would convince them to let me out. Fortunately, another staff member came by that recognized me, otherwise I might have been there an uncomfortably long time. It occurred to me that the locked door, and who decided who was "crazy," was as much a part of the conditions creating insanity as the symptoms of the individual patients¹.

个体与更大系统之间关系的重要性，有一天在我正准备离开那家私立精神病院时，变得可怕地清晰。我走到那扇上锁的门前，说我准备回家了。然而，在我待在那儿期间，门口的工作人员已经换了班，没人有认出我。作为志愿者，我没有官方徽章或任何证明文件。他们以为我是精神病人，不肯放我出去。当我试图解释我是志愿者时，他们只是点头说：「当然，当然，你当然是。」我说的任何话都无法说服他们放我走。幸好另一位认得我的工作人员经过，否则我可能要在那儿待上令人不安的很久。我突然意识到，那扇上锁的门，以及由谁来决定谁是「疯子」，与个体病人的症状一样，都是制造疯狂的情境的一部分¹。

During this period, I also befriended Bateson's personal secretary Judith van Slooten, who had been instrumental in helping to prepare many of Bateson's major works including Steps to an Ecology of Mind and Mind and Nature. I spent quite a number of hours with her in Bateson's office at Kresge College (and other places around Santa Cruz) discussing Bateson's ideas and their implications. She also provided me with copies of Bateson's lesser-known publications and transcripts of presentations he had made that were not publicly available.

在此期间，我还与贝特森的私人秘书朱迪丝·范·斯洛滕（Judith van Slooten）成了朋友，她曾协助准备贝特森的许多重要著作，包括《走向心智生态学》与《心智与自然》（Mind and Nature）。我在克瑞斯吉学院的贝特森办公室（以及圣克鲁兹周边其他地方）与她共度了不少时光，讨论贝特森的思想及其意涵。她还为我提供了贝特森那些鲜为人知的出版物，以及他未公开演讲的文字记录。

最后一道谜题A Final Puzzle

In the last years of his life Gregory Bateson lived at the Esalen Institute in Big Sur California; one of the epicenters of the human potential movement in the 1960s and 70s. Bateson became a "scholar in residence" at Esalen from 1978-80, giving lectures on "Ecology of Mind" and working on a manuscript that would form the basis of his final book Angels Fear: Towards an Epistemology of the Sacred, written in collaboration with his daughter Mary Catherine Bateson.

格里高里·贝特森生命的最后几年，住在加州大苏尔（Big Sur）的伊莎兰学院（Esalen Institute）——1960 与 70 年代人类潜能运动的核心地带之一。1978 至 1980 年间，贝特森成为伊莎兰的「驻院学者」（scholar in residence），主讲「心智生态学」，并着手一部将构成他最后著作《天使的恐惧：走向一种神圣的认识论》（Angels Fear: Towards an Epistemology of the Sacred）基础的文稿，该书与其女儿玛丽·凯瑟琳·贝特森（Mary Catherine Bateson）合著。

The last time I personally saw Bateson was in the cafeteria at Esalen in late 1978. I was at the institute to present one of the first official workshops on NeuroLinguistic Programming (at the age of 23) with my then training partner Terrence McClendon. I mentioned to Bateson that I had been a student of his and started a conversation about the differences between "logical levels" and "logical types" and how they interacted.

我个人最后一次见到贝特森，是 1978 年秋在伊莎兰的食堂。我当时在学院里，与当时的训练伙伴特伦斯·麦克伦登（Terrence McClendon）一起主讲最早几场官方 NLP 工作坊之一（时年 23 岁）。我向贝特森提到我曾是他学生，并开启了关于「逻辑层次」与「逻辑类型」之别、以及它们如何互动的对话。

As a result of this conversation, as well as my study of Bateson's Steps to an Ecology of Mind and other works, I completed a paper (published as Part I of Roots of NLP, 1983) presenting my understanding of the dynamics between

作为这次对话，以及我对贝特森《走向心智生态学》及其他著作研读的结果，我完成了一篇论文（后作&#x4E3A;《NLP 之根》第一部分，1983 年出版），呈现了我对

〔图 p138〕

logical levels and logical types and how it related to brain function. My essential thesis was that, as Bateson asserted, phenomena at different logical levels had a hierarchical relationship – ie, higher levels both integrated but also influenced the expression of lower levels. However, phenomena of different logical types could co-exist without directly influencing each other.

作为说明，考虑以下图示。光波与声波是不同的逻辑类型。它们可以占据同一时空而不相互影响。然而，两道声波或两道光波是同一逻辑类型，会彼此影响，产生干涉图样。但是，若通过特定的电路，将一道波置于比另一道更高的层次上——如调幅（AM）广播传输那样——较高层次的波会调制较低层次的波。

As an illustration, consider the following diagrams. Light waves and sound waves are different logical types. They can occupy the same time and space without affecting one other. Two sound waves or two light waves, however, are the same logical type and will have an impact on each other, creating an interference pattern. If, however, using a particular circuitry, one wave is placed on a higher level than the other, such as in AM radio transmission, the higher-level wave modulates the lower-level wave.

  • 声波 A
  • 光波 A
  • → 声波 A + 光波 B
  • 声波 A
  • 声波 B（同一逻辑层次）
  • → 声波 A + B 干涉
  • 波 A
  • 波 B（较低逻辑层次）
  • → 波 A + 波 B / A 调制 B

〔图 p137〕

  • Sound Wave A
  • Light Wave A
  • → Sound Wave A + Light Wave B

左右枕叶与中央皮层的脑电图记录

  • Sound Wave A
  • Sound Wave B (Same Logical level)
  • → Sound Wave A + B Interference

我将以上想法整理成一份手稿草稿，于 1979 年夏寄给在伊莎兰的贝特森。他回了如下的信，日期为 7 月 23 日。

  • Wave A
  • Wave B (Lower logical level)
  • → Wave A + Wave B / A Modulates B

亲爱的罗伯特：

My hypothesis was that the human nervous system had similar dynamics. My own studies of the human nervous system and research with brain waves (Part II of Roots of NLP) had convinced me that there were both different logical types and different logical levels of activity taking place in our subjective experiences and consequently within our internal wiring. For example, the following EEG recording, made during my study of the relationship between EEG (brain) waves, sensory representational systems and eye movements, shows what appears to be both interference patterns and modulated wave forms.

谢谢你让我看了你手稿中的一部分。麻烦在于，逻辑类型化（logical typing）必须至少考虑到两种相关的依赖类型。这是关于信息的指涉物（referent），以及该指涉物如何作为「整体」或「部分」等，与其他可能的指涉物相关联的问题。然后还有信息如何与思考网络的其他部分相关联的问题。

〔图 p136〕

EEG Recordings of Left and Right Hemispheres of the Occipital and Central Cortex

再次感谢，你真诚的，格里高里·贝特森

I organized these ideas into a draft of a manuscript that I sent to Bateson at Esalen in the summer of 1979. He sent the following reply, dated July 23rd.

尽管贝特森这封回信的语言极为凝练、抽象层级很高，但它清楚地展示了他思考策略那种深刻的系统性本质，以及他所能容纳的复杂程度。对有些人而言，这封回信可能抽象得近乎难以理解。然而，它实际上是对我的假说一次非常精确的回应，自此不断激发着我的思考。他指出，做出逻辑类型与逻辑层次这样的区分，必然是复杂的、相对于系统的其他部分，并且需要多重观点的整合。

Dear Robert:

事实上，仅这两段文字就折射出三项关键的天才模式：

Thank you for showing me a piece of your own manuscript. The trouble is that logical typing has to be done with at least two types of related reliance in mind. This is the question of the referent of the message and how that referent is related to other possible referents as "Whole" or "part" etc. And then there is the question of how the message is related to other parts of the thinking network.

  • 运用多重观点，容纳不同的视角。
  • 在不同归类大小（chunk sizes）与思维层次之间来回移动。
  • 系统性地思考，并聚焦于「表层结构」背后的「深层结构」。

The outside system of referents may be hierarchic in structure but so also the internal network may be hierarchic, and the two systems may not be mutually isomorphic.

除了花时间阅读我的手稿并如此深思地回应之外，贝特森还为我指引了一个方向，并交给我一道有力的谜题——四十余年来，我一直在试图解答。他的反思推动着我对原初思想的理解与应用持续演化，最终导向我发展出所谓的神经逻辑学习与改变层次（Neuro-Logical Levels of Learning and Change）——这将是本章后面部分的焦点。

Thank you again, Yours sincerely, Gregory Bateson

在写作此刻，近半个世纪以来，我养成了一个习惯：几乎每年至少看一次这封信，并且总会有新的发现。

〔图 p136〕

While Bateson's language in this response is very dense and at a high level of abstraction, it clearly illustrates the deeply systemic nature of his thinking strategy and the level of complexity he was able to entertain. To some, the response may seem so abstract as to be almost incomprehensible. Yet it is actually a very precise response to my hypothesis that has stimulated my own thinking ever since. He is pointing out that making such distinctions as logical types and logical levels is necessarily complex, relative to other parts of a system and requires the integration of multiple perspectives.

我在本章余下部分的目标，是尽我所能去拆解这个谜题、让贝特森的策略及其深刻而具转化性的能力，为读者所及。

In fact, just these two paragraphs reflect three of the key patterns of genius:

  • Engaging multiple perspectives and entertaining different points of view.
  • Moving back and forth between different chunk sizes and levels of thinking.
  • Thinking systemically and focusing on the "deep structure" beyond the "surface structure."

Beyond taking the time to read my manuscript and respond so thoughtfully, Bateson pointed me in a direction and presented me with a powerful puzzle that I have been working to solve for the past 45 plus years. His reflections have contributed to a constant evolution of my understanding and application of my original ideas that have led to my development of the so-called Neuro-Logical Levels of Learning and Change – which will be the focus of a later section of this chapter.

I have made it a habit to look at this message at least once a year for almost five decades at the time of this writing and always discover something new as a result.

Gregory Bateson died less than a year after sending me this message on July 4, 1980, at age 76, in the guest house of the San Francisco Zen Center.

My goal for the remainder of this chapter is to do what I can to unpack Bateson's strategies and make their deep and transformational capacity accessible to readers.

连接之模式The Pattern Which Connects

The foundation of Bateson's strategy of genius was his constant search for what he called "the pattern which connects." In his book Mind and Nature, he posed such questions as:

贝特森天才策略的基础，是他对所谓「连接之模式」的不断探寻。在《心智与自然》一书中，他提出了这样的问题：

What pattern connects the crab to the lobster and the orchid to the primrose and all the four of them to me? And me to you? And all the six of us to the amoeba in one direction and to the back-ward schizophrenic in another? What is the pattern which connects all the living creatures?

何种模式将螃蟹与龙虾相连，将兰花与报春花相连，并将这四方与我相连？又将我与汝相连？而我们六者，又如何一方连向阿米巴，另一方连向倒退型精神分裂者？连接所有生物的模式是什么？

〔图 p135〕

What pattern connects the crab to the lobster and the orchid to the primrose?

类似于亚里士多德对「第一性原理」的探寻，贝特森寻求发现所有系统——尤其是生命系统——之中那些更深层、潜在的模式。这些便是诸如对称、重复与节奏等区分。他宣称：「连接之模式是一种元模式（metapattern）。它是模式的模式……首要地，是一场互动部件的舞蹈。」作为说明，他指出：

Similar to Aristotle's search for "first principles" Bateson sought to discover the deeper underlying patterns in all systems – especially living systems. These were distinctions such as symmetry, repetition and rhythm. He claimed, "The pattern which connects is a metapattern. It is a pattern of patterns… primarily a dance of interacting parts." As an illustration, he pointed out:

螃蟹的解剖是重复而富有节奏的。它，如同音乐，是带着调制的重复。事实上，从头部朝向尾部的方向，对应于时间中的一段序列：在胚胎学中，

The anatomy of the crab is repetitive and rhythmical. It is, like music, repetitive with modulation. Indeed, the direction from head toward tail corresponds to a sequence in time: In embryology, the

头部比尾部更古老。从前往后，信息得以流动。

head is older than the tail. A flow of information is possible, from front to rear.

在这些更深层的模式层面上，贝特森相信所有系统都共享着重要的共性。正如他所说：「万物皆为彼此之隐喻。」

At these deeper levels of patterns Bateson believed all systems shared important commonalities. As he put it, "Everything is a metaphor for everything else."

万物皆为彼此之隐喻Everything is a Metaphor for Everything Else

Bateson's constant search for the pattern which connects led to the development of one of the most significant aspects of his mental strategy for exploration and problem solving – a highly developed ability to think using the process of metaphor or analogy to cross the boundaries typically encountered between the sciences. In his book Steps to an Ecology of Mind Bateson describes how, as a young man he:

贝特森对连接之模式的不断探寻，发展出了他探索与解决问题心智策略中最重要的面向之一——一种高度发达的、运用隐喻或类比跨越科学之间典型边界的思考能力。在《走向心智生态学》中，贝特森描述了自己年轻时如何：

… picked up a vague mystical feeling that we must look for the same sort of processes in all fields of natural phenomena – that we might expect to find the same sort of laws at work in the structure of a crystal as in the structure of society, or that the segmentation of an earthworm might really be comparable to the process by which basalt pillars are formed. (p. 74)

……生出一种模糊的神秘感受，认为我们必须在所有自然现象的领域里寻找同一类过程——我们或许会在晶体的结构中，与社会结构中，发现同样类型的法则在运作；或者蚯蚓的体节，或许真的可与玄武岩柱的形成过程相比拟。（p. 74）

In this statement, Bateson describes the essence of his creativity as a guiding feeling that the structure of different phenomena was the same at some deep level. These types of 'gut feelings' are often at the root of creativity. Albert Einstein, for instance, reported having a similar type of guiding feeling during the time he was pursuing the theory of relativity.

在这段陈述中，贝特森描述了他创造力的本质：一种引导性的感受，认为不同现象的结构在某一深层上是相同的。这类「直觉感受」往往是创造力的根源。例如，阿尔伯特·爱因斯坦就曾报告，在追求相对论期间，有过类似类型的引导性感受。

It is obvious that Bateson's "vague mystical feeling" and the attempt to find the similarities between the phenomena of different fields were the foundation of his work in systems theory and cybernetics. Both seek to map out the commonalities between the basic processes of all systems. Bateson's search for the general laws of "natural phenomena," however, also led him to another important discovery:

显然，贝特森的「模糊神秘感受」以及寻找不同领域现象之间相似性的尝试，是他系统理论与控制论工作的基础。两者都试图勾勒出所有系统基本过程之间的共性。然而，贝特森对「自然现象」普遍法则的探寻，也引他走向另一重要发现：

… that the types of mental operation which are useful in analyzing one field may be equally useful in another – that the framework of science, rather than the framework of Nature, is the same in all fields." (p. 74)

……分析某一领域有用的心智运作类型，在另一领域可能同样有用——也就是说，科学的框架，而非自然的框架，在所有领域都是相同的。（p. 74）

In other words, the 'laws' of science were more in the mental strategies for how one thought about Nature rather than in Nature itself. Since we can only know the world through the perceptions, we have of it and the mental maps or models we make of it, it is as important to study the structure of these maps of the world as it is to study the world itself. The sciences of 'physics', 'psychology' or 'anthropology' are different models of the world that are derived from the mental strategies for experiencing the world used by the people within those fields. A physicist, psychologist and anthropologist could all examine the same external phenomenon but would have completely different perceptions and conclusions. Bateson realized that an anthropologist's mental strategy might help a psychiatrist or physicist and vice versa.

换言之，「科学」的法则，更多存在于人如何思考自然的心智策略之中，而非自然本身。既然我们只能通过我们所拥有的感知、以及我们所制造的关于世界的心理地图或模型来认识世界，那么研究这些世界观地图的结构，与研究世界本身同等重要。物理学、心理学或人类学，都是派生自那些领域内部之人所使用之经验世界的心智策略的不同世界观模型。一位物理学家、心理学家与人类学家可以审视同一个外部现象，却会有完全不同的感知与结论。贝特森意识到，一位人类学家的心智策略，或许能帮到一位精神科医生或物理学家，反之亦然。

The ability to use the "mental operations" from one field to analyze problems in a different field was undoubtedly the source of some of Bateson's most creative inspirations. By applying biological thinking to the

将某一领域的「心智运作」用于分析另一领域的问题，无疑正是贝特森某些最具创造力之灵感的来源。通过将生物学思维应用于

field of anthropology, anthropological thinking to the field of psychology, psychological thinking to the field of biology and so on, Bateson was able to contribute unique innovative solutions that specialists in only one field could never have produced.

人类学领域、将人类学思维应用于心理学领域、将心理学思维应用于生物学领域，如此类推，贝特森得以贡献出专精于单一领域的专家永远无法产生的独特创新解法。

贝特森运用连接之模式的策略结构The Structure of Bateson's Strategy for Applying the Pattern which Connects

An important feature of Bateson's cross-field thinking strategy is that it is able to move between abstract concepts and concrete applications. As he explained:

贝特森跨领域思维策略的一个重要特征，是它能在抽象概念与具体应用之间移动。正如他所解释的：

When we compare a social problem with a problem of animal differentiation we are at once provided with a visual diagram in terms of which we are able to talk a little more precisely. (p.76)

当我们将一个社会问题与动物分化的问题相比较时，我们立刻获得了一个视觉图表，得以借此更精确地谈论一些事情。（p.76）

[This kind of] vague 'hunch' derived from some other science leads into the precise formulations of the other science in terms of which it is possible to think more fruitfully about our own material. (p. 79)

［这类］源自其他科学的模糊「预感」，会导入另一科学中更精确的定式，藉此得以对我们自己的材料做更有成效的思考。（p. 79）

Thus, the strategy Bateson is describing for how he adapts the mode of thinking from one field to the contents of another field involves:

因此，贝特森所描述的、将某一领域的思维方式调适于另一领域之内容所在的策略，涉及：

  1. an internal feeling (K) that leads him to make
  2. a visual analogy or metaphor (V) that can then be translated into
  3. the specific language (A_d) of the particular field in which he is trying to bring new insights.
  1. 一种内在感受（K），引导他做出
  2. 一个视觉的类比或隐喻（V），随后可被转译为
  3. 他试图带入新洞见的那个特定领域的专门语言（A_d）。

The level of detail becomes more finely chunked and more precise with each step in the process – a) the feeling is "vague," b) the picture provides a more concrete "diagram" from which c) "precise" words may be formulated. The more abstract and general visual analogy allows one to swap strategies between different fields. The specific scientific language allows one to chunk the diagram into a form that is usable in that field.

过程中的每一步，细节都变得更精细归类（chunked）、更精确——a) 感受是「模糊的」，b) 图像提供了更具体的「图表」，由此 c) 可定出「精确」的词语。更抽象、更一般的视觉类比，让人在不同领域间交换策略成为可能。特定的科学语言，则让人能把图表归类成该领域可用的形式。

It is the stage of moving to language where Bateson's sensitivity to nominalizations (words which turn processes into things) in relation to cause and effect was most active. He stated:

正是在迈向语言的这一阶段，贝特森对那些关涉因果的名词化（将过程变成事物的词）最为敏感。他指出：

Words are dangerous things… We are all familiar with this loose use of words in such phrases as: "the causes of war are economic," "economic behavior," "he was influenced by his emotions," "his symptoms are the result of conflict between his superego and his id"… Psychoanalysis has erred sadly in using words that are too short and therefore appear more concrete than they are.

词语是危险的东西……我们都熟悉这类词语的松散用法，例如：「战争的原因是经济的」「经济行为」「他受情绪影响」「他的症状是他超我与本我冲突的结果」……精神分析在运用过短、因而显得比实际更具体的词语上，犯了可悲的错误。

"Economic behavior," "emotion," "superego" and "id" are all nominalizations. They are complex processes made up of a network of relationships that have been generalized into a single entity. The examples that Bateson quotes above also all imply a type of linear cause and effect, which he also considered to be potentially dangerous. He strongly believed that, from a cybernetic perspective, "no part of such an internally interactive system can have unilateral control over any other part." As we will see, Bateson's attention to these types of "dangers" was a key part of his genius.

「经济行为」「情绪」「超我」与「本我」都是名词化。它们是由关系网络概括而成的单一实体所构成的复杂过程。贝特森上面所引的例子，也都暗含了一种线性的因果，而他同样认为这可能是危险的。他强烈相信，从控制论的视角看，「如此内部互动的系统，没有任何部分能对任何其他部分拥有单边的控制。」正如我们将看到的，贝特森对这些「危险」的留意，是他天才的关键部分。

Bateson summarized his overall process in the following way:

贝特森以如下方式总结他的整体过程：

My pyschical view of phenomena contributed specifically to build up this double habit of mind – it led me into wild 'hunches' and, at the same time, compelled more formal thinking about those hunches. It encouraged looseness of thought and then immediately insisted that looseness be measured up against a rigid concreteness. The point is that the first hunch from analogy is wild, and then, the moment I begin to work out the analogy, I am brought up against rigid formulations which have been devised in the field from which I borrow. (p. 75)

我的现象心理观，具体地促成了这双重思维习惯的养成——它引我走向狂野的「预感」，同时，又迫使我对那些预感做更形式化的思考。它鼓励思想的松散，然后又立刻坚持，要用僵硬的具体性去量度那份松散。关键在于，类比的第一个预感是狂野的，而随后，当我开始推演那类比时，我撞上了从我借用之处那领域中所设计出的僵硬定式。（p. 75）

Bateson's strategy involved the balance and integration of two different levels of chunking: (1) a more general, simultaneous, metaphoric or analogical level of thinking that is primarily kinesthetic and visual (right-brain oriented) and (2) a more precise, sequential, analytical level of thinking that is primarily verbal (left-brain oriented).

贝特森的策略，涉及两种不同归类层次的平衡与整合：(1) 一种更一般、同时性、隐喻或类比层面的思考，主要动觉且视觉（右脑取向）；(2) 一种更精确、序列性、分析层面的思考，主要言语（左脑取向）。

This metaphorically oriented thinking strategy has been a major resource used by many of history's most creative scientists. The great inventor Thomas Edison, for example, evidently got the idea for motion pictures by making an analogy from his invention of the phonograph, claiming, "I am experimenting upon an instrument which does for the eye what the phonograph does for the ear." Albert Einstein claimed that he thought almost exclusively in terms of such metaphoric images or "special imaginary constructions" in order to develop his concepts of relativity, quantum theory and four-dimensional space.

这种以隐喻为导向的思维策略，是历史上许多最具创造力的科学家所使用的主要资源。例如，大发明家托马斯·爱迪生显然是从他的留声机发明中做类比，才得到电影的点子——他声称：「我正在试验一种仪器，它之于眼睛，正如留声机之于耳朵。」阿尔伯特·爱因斯坦宣称，他几乎完全以这类隐喻性意象或「特殊的想象性建构」来思考，以发展出他的相对论、量子理论与四维空间概念。

「溯因」对「演绎」思维"Abductive" Versus "Deductive" Thinking

Bateson referred to this type of metaphorical thinking as abductive logic in contrast with deductive or inductive logic. He illustrated the difference between this abductive way of thinking and the typical deductive logic of science by contrasting the following statements:

贝特森将这类隐喻性思维称为溯因逻辑（abductive logic），以区别于演绎或归纳逻辑。他通过对比以下陈述，说明了这种溯因思维与科学的典型演绎逻辑之间的差别：

〔图 p132〕

Deductive Thinking

  • 人皆有一死
  • 苏格拉底是人
  • 苏格拉底将会死
  • Men die
  • Socrates is a man
  • Socrates will die

溯因思维

〔图 p132〕

Abductive Thinking

演绎与溯因思维过程之比较

  • Men die
  • Grass dies
  • Men are grass

贝特森指出，第一个三段论是经典的「正确」逻辑之例，构成了科学理论的基础；而第二个陈述被认为是错误且「不合逻辑」的，并与精神分裂者与疯子的思维相关联。他略带讽刺地补充道：「我大多数的思考都采取第二种序列的形式。」他主张，第一种逻辑聚焦于客体与范畴，而非结构与关系，可能导致我们思维的僵化。第二种则导向更具创造力的思考，并可能实际上引领我们发现关于现实的更深真理。

Comparison of Deductive and Abductive Thinking Processes

事实上，贝特森甚至宣称：「隐喻不仅仅是漂亮的诗；它既非好逻辑也非坏逻辑，而实际上是生物世界据以被建构的逻辑。」

Bateson pointed out that the first syllogism is an example of the classical "correct" logic that forms the basis for scientific theory, whereas the second statement is considered incorrect and 'illogical' and has been associated the thinking of schizophrenics and the insane. He added wryly that, "most of my thinking takes the form of the second kind of sequence." He contended that the first type of logic focuses on objects and categories rather than structure and relationship and can cause a rigidity in our thinking. The second type leads to more creative thinking and may actually lead us to discover deeper truths about reality.

贝特森最爱的轶事之一，是关于一位科学家问超级计算机：「你算得出自己有一天会像人类一样思考吗？」机器开始分析自己的计算习惯。经过大量处理后，计算机终于回答：「这让我想起一个故事……」

In fact, Bateson went so far as to claim that "metaphor was not just pretty poetry, it was not either good or bad logic but was in fact the logic upon which the biological world had been built."

对贝特森而言，溯因与演绎这两种思维方式的平衡，对科学的推进至关重要。

One of Bateson's favorite anecdotes was about the scientist who asked the supercomputer, "Do you compute that you will ever think like a human being?" The machine set to work to analyze its own computational habits. After much processing the computer finally answered, "That reminds me of a story…"

［每当我们开始过分坚持「操作主义」或符号逻辑，或任何其他这些极其重要的［科学］系统时……我们便丧失了一些思考新想法的能力。而同样地，当然，每当我们反叛于形式化思维与阐述的僵化空洞，任由想法狂奔，我们也同样在丧失。在我看来，科学思想的进展来自松散与严格思考的结合，而这结合是科学最珍贵的工具。（p. 75）

〔图 p131〕

To Bateson, the balance between these two different ways of thinking, abductive and deductive, was crucial to the advancement of science.

事实上，平衡这两种过程的策略，似乎是历史上许多伟大心智（如爱因斯坦与爱迪生）之天才的重要元素。无疑，它也是贝特森诸多贡献的基础。正如他所说：「我们应当接纳并享受科学思想这种双重本性，并愿意重视这两个过程协力给予我们对世界理解之进展的方式。」遗憾的是，第二种（隐喻性）思维方式，至今仍未被作为科学教育的一部分来鼓励。事实上，它被许多教师视为「不科学」。即便是 NLP 的学生，也常忘记结构之双重描述与直觉思维之间这一重要的平衡。

[W]henever we start insisting too hard upon 'operationalism' or symbolic logic or any other of these very essential [scientific] systems… we lose something of the ability to think new thoughts. And equally, of course, whenever we rebel against the sterile rigidity of formal thought and exposition and let our ideas run wild, we likewise lose. As I see it, the advances in scientific thought come from a combination of loose and strict thinking, and this combination is the most precious tool of science. (p. 75)

Bateson concluded that "rigor alone is paralytic death, but imagination alone is insanity."

The strategy of balancing these two processes, indeed, seems to be an important element of the genius of many of the great minds in history like Einstein and Edison. Certainly, it was the basis of many of Bateson's contributions. As he said, "We ought to accept and enjoy this dual nature of scientific thought and be willing to value the way in which the two processes work together to give us advances in understanding of the world."
